16:I[44692,["/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/08cykig6vtx_p.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/07qi2okp-.yp9.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0frujd.eyhz4o.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0n4z.7yu76je-.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/09-o.77-p0p36.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/1304s0wcp2kce.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/0j1uy9t~zxcf0.js","/paper-daily-site/_next/static/chunks/14_5o-nbg8k_r.js"],"DailyView"]
17:T4ff,The analysis uses the online-to-nonconvex conversion framework. The optimizer’s update rule is treated as an online learner, and the learner’s regret is converted into a Goldstein-style stationarity guarantee for nonsmooth nonconvex objectives. For a momentum matrix M = U Sigma V^T, the ideal Muon direction is the polar factor U V^T, which preserves singular subspaces and maps all positive singular values to one. This hard singular-value map is discontinuous. Finite Newton–Schulz replaces it with a polynomial map obtained by q iterations of an odd matrix polynomial, implemented with matrix multiplications rather than an SVD. The paper shows that this finite-depth map is the gradient of a smoothed spectral potential evaluated at the momentum matrix. This turns Muon into a gradient-based prediction algorithm. The discounted regret decomposes into a penalty term and a stability term. The penalty decreases as the smoothed potential approaches the nuclear norm; the stability term is controlled by the Lipschitz constant of the finite Newton–Schulz map. Choosing q = O(log(1/epsilon)) balances these terms. The key conceptual move is to use the finite-depth deviation from the exact polar factor as smoothing, rather than treating it only as approximation error.18:T448,The core metric is Token Economy Score, TES(Mr, Mb, T) = [Acc(Mr,T) - Acc(Mb,T)] / [GenTok(Mr,T) / GenTok(Mb,T)]. Mr is the reasoning-enabled model, Mb is the non-reasoning baseline, and GenTok is reasoning tokens plus final answer tokens. Input tokens are deliberately excluded because they are mostly determined by the benchmark prompt and evaluation harness rather than by the model’s reasoning behavior. TES is therefore a marginal metric, unlike absolute accuracy-per-token metrics such as Per-Token Intelligence. TES greater than 1 means the accuracy gain in percentage points exceeds the generated-token multiplier; values between 0 and 1 indicate positive but weak marginal efficiency; values at or below 0 indicate that reasoning is wasteful or damaging. The paper also defines deployment-facing quantities, including Reasoning Cost Share and Deployment Cost Multiplier, to connect token behavior with cloud and on-premises cost profiles. A key caveat is explicit: TES is a practical convention, not a universal utility function, and should be read with baseline and absolute accuracy.19:T4e7,The method, VIG-Sampler, is built on two empirical observations. First, candidate tokens that place stronger attention on image tokens tend to be both better grounded in the visual input and more useful for predicting the remaining masked tokens. Based on this, the sampler uses token-level image attention as the ordering score, prioritizing visually informative tokens over high-confidence but semantically weak ones. Second, when multiple tokens are decoded in parallel, their individual information gains do not add up cleanly because some of them are redundant. The paper argues that this redundancy is reflected in similar token-to-image attention distributions. VIG-Sampler therefore adds a similarity-based penalty during subset selection: if a candidate attends to the image in a way that is too similar to already selected tokens, its priority is reduced. This gives a simple two-part design: image-attention-based ranking for grounding, plus attention-similarity penalty for complementary information. Compared with certainty-based samplers, it avoids locking in punctuation, copulas, or other frequent low-content tokens too early. Compared with text-only info-gain samplers, it explicitly injects visual evidence into the decoding trajectory.1a:Ta22,方法核心是“自参考的局部分支探索 + signed distillation”。在某个 on-policy 时间步 t_j，模型先按当前 ODE 预测得到一个确定性的下一状态；然后从同一个父状态出发，额外分出 K 个带噪声的 SDE 候选分支。每个分支只在当前步做随机探索，之后再用确定性 ODE 一路 rollout 到干净图像。这样做的目的很明确：把当前局部方向的好坏，转化为最终图像奖励的差异。

为了把终局奖励变成局部监督，作者同时从同一父状态做一条 deterministic ODE rollout 作为 baseline。每个分支最终得到的 reward 与 baseline reward 做比较，形成 normalized branch advantage。这样 advantage 不是绝对分数，而是“从当前状态出发，这个局部扰动比默认走法更好还是更差”。这一步很关键，因为它把不同 prompt、不同 reward 量纲、不同时间步上的分数差异压成了可比较的局部相对信号。

训练目标不是只拉近最好分支，而是 all-branch pull-push distillation。高 advantage 分支把当前 velocity field 拉向更优局部方向；低 advantage 分支则把模型从差方向上推开。相比只学 best-of-K，这种 signed objective 利用了更多探索样本，样本效率更高，也更像一个局部排序学习问题。但 signed update 容易不稳，尤其是“推开差分支”可能与“靠近好分支”方向冲突。为此作者加了 direction-aware attenuation：如果某个 repulsive update 会明显抵消 best branch 的主方向，就衰减其权重，避免训练在局部几何上自相矛盾。

另一个技术点是 SDE-variance normalization。因为不同时间步、不同噪声强度下，SDE 分支的位移尺度不同，若直接回归分支目标，loss 尺度会失衡。作者用 SDE 方差做归一化，使每步监督更接近统一量纲，并给出与 reward-tilted KL 最小化相关的解释：模型在每个局部状态附近，把概率质量往高奖励分支倾斜。

多目标部分也值得注意。已有一些 OPD 方法会为不同目标训练不同 teacher，再在 field level 做路由或融合。Self-OPD 不这么做。它先把多个 reward 归一化后在 scalar reward 层融合，只用融合后的分数对局部分支排序；真正回归的对象仍然是某条具体分支对应的 velocity target，而不是多个 teacher field 的线性组合。这样避免了不同目标梯度直接打架，也更符合“同一张图同时满足多个约束”的目标，而不是不同 teacher 各自擅长不同样本。1b:T953,The method is built around self-referenced local branching plus signed distillation. At an on-policy step t_j, the current model first predicts the deterministic next state along the ODE trajectory. From the same parent state, it then spawns K stochastic SDE branches. Each branch explores only locally at the current step, after which it is rolled out deterministically to a clean image. The purpose is to evaluate whether a local directional perturbation leads to a better final sample.

A deterministic ODE rollout from the same parent state serves as a self-reference baseline. Final rewards from the K branches are compared against this baseline reward to produce normalized branch advantages. This is important because the signal becomes relative and local: not whether a sample is globally good, but whether a local move from the current state is better or worse than the default trajectory.

Training uses an all-branch pull-push distillation objective. Positive-advantage branches pull the velocity field toward better local directions, while negative-advantage branches push it away from poor ones. Compared with only regressing toward the best branch, this uses more of the sampled neighborhood and turns local exploration into a richer ranking signal. The downside is potential instability: repulsive updates can conflict with attractive ones. To address that, the paper introduces direction-aware attenuation, which downweights repulsion when it would substantially cancel the direction of the best branch.

Another key component is SDE-variance normalization. Since branch displacement scales vary across timesteps and noise levels, direct regression would produce poorly calibrated losses. Normalizing by SDE variance makes the per-step target more comparable across the trajectory and is connected in the paper to a reward-tilted KL view: locally shifting probability mass toward higher-reward branches.

For multi-objective alignment, Self-OPD avoids field-level fusion of multiple teachers. Instead, it normalizes and combines scalar rewards first, uses the composite reward only to rank branches, and still regresses toward a concrete trajectory-derived velocity target. This is cleaner for black-box rewards and better aligned with the actual goal: one image satisfying multiple constraints simultaneously, rather than separate experts excelling on different samples.1c:T55d,The experimental story, based on the provided full-text sections, is centered on single-reward and mixed-reward alignment, with comparisons against two families of baselines: direct RL methods and teacher-based OPD methods. The paper reports that Self-OPD outperforms both, with the advantage being especially visible in mixed-reward settings where one model is expected to satisfy text rendering, compositional correctness, and aesthetics at the same time.

The most informative comparisons are likely the following: (1) single-reward versus mixed-reward performance, to test whether the method really resolves objective conflict; (2) training stability against teacher-free RL, to validate the dense-supervision claim; (3) performance against teacher-based OPD, to test whether removing the teacher sacrifices or improves alignment quality; and (4) ablations on branch count K, pull-only versus pull-push, direction-aware attenuation, and reward fusion strategy.

The excerpt does not include the full result tables, model sizes, compute budget, or exact benchmark numbers, so it would be unsafe to invent precise gains. The numbers worth checking in the full paper are the reward improvements over RL baselines, whether all component rewards improve simultaneously under mixed objectives, and whether removing push or attenuation causes clear degradation or instability.1d:T44c,GRAS combines two changes. First, it improves the guided proposal. Prior GILC-style methods correct clean-token logits with a straight-through reward gradient, but the gradient is often estimated from a single noisy sample. For differentiable rewards, GRAS uses a Gumbel-Rao / Rao-Blackwellized reveal estimator to analytically average part of the reveal randomness and reduce variance. For non-differentiable rewards, it uses a leave-one-out baseline to reduce particle-level reward noise. These proposal changes add no denoiser calls. Second, it changes the search stage. Standard SMC/TDS-like resampling often uses a fixed temperature, although the spread of values differs across denoising steps. GRAS introduces AMC, an adaptive-temperature resampling rule that standardizes per-step values into group-relative advantages and lets a single scale parameter control selection pressure. Compared with simply stacking gradient guidance and search, the useful contribution is the accounting framework: variance reduction, adaptive selection, diversity cost, and denoiser budget are analyzed together.1e:T7e8,The proposed solution is RecursiveGPT, built around two ideas. The first is factorized embeddings, which reduce the share of parameters spent on the input and output vocabulary maps. This matters because when hidden size is reduced, Transformer block parameters shrink roughly quadratically, while embedding-related parameters shrink much more slowly. As a result, small standard LMs can spend a disproportionate fraction of capacity on token lookup rather than sequence computation.

The second idea is recursive weight sharing across depth. Instead of stacking many independently parameterized Transformer blocks, the model repeatedly applies a shared block for multiple recurrent steps. This increases the amount of computation performed on each token without a proportional increase in parameter count. The paper is explicit that this is not mainly a compute-efficiency argument in the serving sense; it is a way to scale computation under fixed data while keeping model size under control.

Architecturally, this is closer to ALBERT-style cross-layer sharing plus recurrent refinement than to a new sequence model family. The interesting questions are therefore in the implementation details: how many recursive steps to use, how to stabilize training with shared blocks and residual connections, whether step information is encoded explicitly, and how to choose the factorization dimension for embeddings. The paper studies these choices systematically in a BabyLM-like setup rather than presenting a single tuned model.

A notable aspect of the work is what it does not rely on. It does not primarily solve overfitting through aggressive recipe engineering such as unusually large weight decay, elaborate stopping heuristics, or late ensembling. Nor does it switch to a different objective such as masked LM or diffusion-style masked modeling. The claim is narrower and cleaner: even with a conventional causal LM objective, architecture alone can improve the compute–parameter trade-off in the limited-data regime.1f:T863,The experiments span roughly 10M to 100M words, model sizes from about 13M to 1.2B parameters, two corpora, and several evaluations including BabyLM-relevant benchmarks such as BLiMP and COMPS, with supplementary reporting on BLiMP Supplement, EWoK, and Entity Tracking. The most important result is not a single score but the shape of the scaling behavior.

First, there is a clear optimal model size, and that optimum depends strongly on both the data budget and the evaluation target. The best size at 10M words is not the same as at 100M words, and different downstream or linguistic evaluations prefer different scales. That is a strong empirical reminder that “bigger is better” does not transfer cleanly into the low-data regime.

Second, the paper shows that standard Transformers use parameters inefficiently when scaled down. As models get smaller, the fraction of parameters tied up in embeddings and the LM head rises sharply, while performance does not improve monotonically with total parameter count. Past a certain point, training loss may continue to decrease while validation and downstream metrics deteriorate. RecursiveGPT improves on matched standard Transformer baselines by allocating more of the budget to token transformation rather than to independent parameters.

Third, RecursiveGPT outperforms standard Transformer baselines at both 10M and 100M word budgets and remains competitive with strong BabyLM 2025 systems. The abstract does not list all numbers, but the body emphasizes consistent gains across the main evaluations. The ablations are important: both factorized embeddings and recursive depth help, but the larger benefit comes from adding computation through shared-depth recurrence rather than merely shrinking vocabulary maps.

Fourth, the training-dynamics analysis is useful. The paper documents the overfitting pattern directly: larger models fit the training corpus better but generalize worse, while recursive models make better use of extra compute under the same data budget. For researchers interested in limited-data scaling, these curves are more informative than the leaderboard comparisons.20:T445,The authors formulate variable-length generation in a padded sequence space with an absorbing empty token, so autoregressive decoding can be viewed as a time-dependent transport from the empty sequence to complete samples. They then define a family of target distributions derived from the base LLM, including powered distributions for sharpening, product distributions for combining constraints, and tilted distributions using additional potentials. Two sampling algorithms are proposed. The Sequential Monte Carlo variant maintains a population of particles along an annealing path and uses importance weights plus resampling to avoid the degeneracy of one-shot importance sampling. The Replica Exchange variant runs chains at different temperatures or sharpness levels and swaps states across replicas, allowing high-temperature chains to explore while low-temperature chains focus on high-probability regions. Compared with Best-of-N, these methods use intermediate distributions and adaptive weighting rather than drawing many independent base-model samples and filtering them afterwards.21:T76c,The main idea is affix-oriented cache reuse. Instead of restricting reuse to shared prefixes, the paper generalizes it to any shared contiguous span, or affix, which may appear at the beginning, middle, or end of the context. This is a natural systems opportunity created by bidirectional attention: unlike AR models, DLLMs are not inherently prefix-centric.

The key challenge is deciding which affix-token KV states become meaningfully stale as the masked generation tokens evolve. ACache introduces Anchor Tokens for this purpose. It measures the cross-attention importance from masked generation tokens to affix tokens, identifies a small request-specific subset of influential affix tokens, and recomputes KV states only for those anchors. The remaining affix-token cache is reused. In effect, the method turns a coarse full-span refresh decision into a sparse token-level refresh decision.

Implementation-wise, ACache is built on top of Fast-dLLM. It replaces periodic full-cache recomputation with selective anchor-token recomputation, so it is an incremental but targeted improvement over existing approximate caching frameworks rather than a completely new inference stack. The authors also implement a shared-prefix prototype in Nano-vLLM to test whether the gains survive under a more realistic serving environment with paged memory management and continuous batching.

Relative to prior work, the distinction is clear. dLLM-Cache uses asymmetric prompt/response refresh and feature-similarity-based partial recomputation for response tokens. dKV-Cache focuses more on decoded-token reuse with delayed block recomputation. Fast-dLLM improves the overall inference framework but still relies on periodic refresh. ACache is novel in treating shared context itself as the optimization target and shrinking the recomputation unit from an entire shared span to a request-specific token subset.22:T504,The main results operate at three levels. First, quality recovery: compared with direct affix-cache reuse, which causes noticeable degradation, ACache recovers most of the lost accuracy across multiple benchmarks while recomputing only around 20% of affix tokens. That number matters because it suggests the stale-cache problem is sparse enough to be exploitable. Second, system gains: in the Nano-vLLM shared-prefix prototype, ACache reduces recomputation latency by up to 55.7% and improves end-to-end throughput by up to 1.68x. This indicates that the benefit is not just a FLOP-level argument but can survive a realistic serving stack. Third, scope: the method applies to shared spans beyond prefixes, which is practically relevant because reusable text in production often appears as retrieved snippets, tool outputs, templates, or summarized dialogue history rather than only as a left prefix.

For a researcher, the most informative part is likely the ablation structure rather than any single headline number: how stable the anchor-selection rule is, how quality changes as the recomputation ratio varies, whether affix position matters, and how stale-cache error accumulates across denoising steps. If the paper includes those plots, they are the ones worth studying closely.23:T598,TwinKV defines importance through pairwise redundancy in key space. For each token, it counts how many non-adjacent tokens have a key vector with cosine similarity above a fixed threshold. A high twin count means the token is redundant; a low count means it is likely unique and harder to recover if evicted. Two design choices matter. First, the method operates on keys rather than values or hidden states, because eviction affects what can later be retrieved through attention, and keys are the retrieval addresses. Second, it excludes adjacent tokens to avoid trivial local similarity from contiguous text.

The more interesting systems idea is to use TwinKV as a repair pass rather than a standalone eviction policy. Given any existing method’s retained and evicted sets under a fixed budget, TwinKV looks for two types of mismatch: an evicted token with no surviving near-duplicate, and a retained token whose information is already duplicated among retained tokens. It then swaps them while keeping the budget unchanged. This is a low-intrusion design: it does not need the wrapped method’s internal scores, only its final keep set. The paper also distinguishes this from anchor-based approaches that score keys by distance from a single global reference point. Those methods measure outlierness relative to a centroid; TwinKV measures substitutability through local pairwise relations. That is a different inductive bias.24:T623,The experiments answer two questions. First, does attention track causal utility? Using chunk-level leave-one-out on real long-context QA, the authors measure how much removing a chunk hurts the log-probability of the correct answer and compare that with the chunk’s attention mass in the original forward pass. The correlation is effectively zero, which directly weakens the premise behind attention-based eviction.

Second, does TwinKV improve existing policies as a post-hoc repair? On Qwen3-4B, the method is composed with four recent eviction baselines: StreamingLLM, PyramidKV, SnapKV, and ExpectedAttention. Evaluation covers LongBench, LooGLE, RULER, and a short-context MMLU-Pro control set, at compression ratios 0.3, 0.5, and 0.7. The gains are conditional rather than universal: TwinKV improves a majority of evaluated cells for StreamingLLM (up to 64% of cells) and PyramidKV (57%), is closer to neutral for SnapKV (41%), and helps only a minority for ExpectedAttention (16%). The paper argues that ExpectedAttention is already near ceiling on several settings, leaving more room for harmful perturbation than for correction. Another consistent pattern is that TwinKV helps more under tighter budgets; at the loosest compression ratio, average gains are smallest. That fits the paper’s causal story: when every retained slot matters, preserving unique information becomes more valuable.

The empirical emphasis is on breadth of positive configurations and compatibility with multiple baselines, not on a single large absolute score jump on one benchmark.25:T49a,Start with the introduction and Figure 1. The most important takeaway is the causal probe against the attention-as-importance assumption; that diagnostic is more memorable than the repair rule itself.

Then read the leave-one-out setup in Section 2 carefully. The paper’s argument depends on how marginal utility is defined, at what chunk granularity, and how the correlation is computed.

Section 3 is the method core. Focus on why the authors choose a swap-based repair pass instead of rescoring all tokens globally, and why pairwise redundancy differs from centroid-distance scoring. If the goal is only to understand the method, this section is enough.

In the experiments, prioritize: the per-baseline win-rate pattern, the dependence on compression ratio, and the MMLU-Pro short-context control. Those results clarify that TwinKV is mainly a long-context memory repair mechanism rather than a general capability booster. A careful skim should take about 20–30 minutes. For deeper reading, inspect threshold sensitivity, the non-adjacency constraint, and any layer/head sensitivity analysis, since those details determine portability to other models and serving stacks.26:T8e2,The paper first performs a systematic diagnosis, then proposes a mitigation method. The study covers two representative MLLMs: Wan2.2 for multimodal generation and Qwen3-VL for multimodal reasoning. It evaluates multiple low-precision formats, including MXFP8, MXFP4, and HiF4, across a wide set of components: vision encoders, text encoders, attention blocks, feed-forward layers, multimodal projection layers, and MoE modules. The analysis shows that FP8 is nearly lossless, while FP4 degradation is highly heterogeneous. In Wan2.2, the visual generation backbone is the main bottleneck and the UMT5 text encoder is relatively robust. In Qwen3-VL, the vision encoder is especially sensitive, though the language backbone also degrades under FP4. Crucially, ablations separating weight-only from activation quantization indicate that activation quantization is the dominant source of performance loss.

Based on this diagnosis, the paper proposes Residual Fallback Quantization (RFQ). Unlike threshold-based outlier fallback methods, which detect large values and route them through a higher-precision path, RFQ uses a fully low-bit and differentiable decomposition. It first quantizes the activation into a primary FP4 representation, then explicitly computes the quantization residual and sends that residual through a secondary low-bit quantization path for reconstruction. Conceptually, the approximation is q(x) + q_res(x - q(x)), rather than a hard split between normal values and outliers.

This design has several practical advantages. It is differentiable and suitable for end-to-end training. It avoids threshold tuning, which is difficult when outlier behavior varies by module and evolves during training. It does not require full-precision fallback, making it more compatible with true FP4 execution. And because it models the current quantization error directly, it is better aligned with dynamic training-time activation statistics than PTQ-style calibration methods.

Methodologically, RFQ sits between static PTQ smoothing and mixed-precision QAT fallback. It accepts that FP4 cannot faithfully represent the full activation range in many MLLM modules, and therefore models the error instead of forcing the original distribution through a single quantizer.27:T644,From the provided full text, several empirical conclusions are already clear.

1. Precision formats separate cleanly in quality: MXFP8 is close to lossless, while MXFP4 and HiF4 incur substantial degradation. This suggests that 8-bit training/deployment is already fairly mature for current MLLMs, whereas 4-bit remains the hard regime.
2. Activation quantization hurts much more than weight quantization. The paper runs extensive ablations to isolate their contributions, and the main takeaway is that compressing weights alone is often manageable, but pushing activations to FP4 causes the major collapse.
3. Sensitivity is strongly module-dependent. In Wan2.2, the WanDiT visual generation backbone is the main failure point, while the text encoder is relatively stable. In Qwen3-VL, the vision encoder is the most sensitive, though language modules are not immune. This argues against a single uniform quantization strategy across the whole MLLM stack.
4. RFQ recovers a meaningful portion of the FP4 loss without using high-precision fallback. The abstract excerpt does not provide exact numbers, but the framing makes clear that RFQ primarily addresses activation-outlier-induced instability rather than offering a small average gain everywhere.

If the full paper includes detailed tables, the most informative numbers to inspect are the relative gaps among FP8, FP4, and HiF4 on both generation and reasoning tasks, and the decomposition into weight-only, activation-only, and joint quantization. For pretraining researchers, those diagnostics are more useful than a single final benchmark score.28:T574,Read this paper with diagnosis first and method second. Start from the experimental setup and module-level ablations before diving into RFQ details.

First, read the introduction and the main experimental tables to confirm that the study spans both generative and reasoning MLLMs rather than a single architecture. That context matters when interpreting which conclusions are likely to transfer.

Second, focus on the activation-versus-weight ablations and the ranking of module sensitivities. This is the highest-information part of the paper because it answers why FP4 fails, not just whether it fails.

Third, move to the RFQ formulation and implementation details. Pay attention to how the residual is quantized, what extra storage or bandwidth it introduces, and what the training and inference overheads are. If your interest is low-precision pretraining, these system costs matter as much as the recovered accuracy.

Fourth, if the paper includes activation histograms, outlier statistics, or layerwise error heatmaps, those are worth careful inspection. They usually explain the mechanism more directly than the final benchmark tables.

As a skim paper, 30 to 45 minutes is enough to capture the main message. If you are working on FP8/FP4 training, multimodal pretraining systems, or hardware-aware model design, it is worth spending 1 to 1.5 hours on the ablations and appendix details.29:T61c,There are two main components. First is dataset construction. Instead of scraping noisy online conversations or using lightly controlled crowdworkers, the authors hire professional script writers to produce complete human-to-human dialogues. The tasks are goal-directed but open-ended, such as tutoring, planning, or everyday assistance. They intentionally avoid heavy knowledge retrieval, coding, or tasks with a single correct endpoint. That design choice matters: if the task is too knowledge-bound, the benchmark becomes another factual QA test; if it is too rigidly task-scripted, it stops measuring natural conversation.

Second is annotation and metric analysis. UPHELD provides dense turn-level labels rather than only conversation-level scores. The labels target dimensions such as stylistic consistency, content consistency, and coherence. This lets the benchmark expose local failures inside a long interaction instead of averaging them away. The paper then compares several evaluation families: classical lexical/semantic metrics, single LLM-as-a-judge systems, and a learned mixture-of-judges built using UPHELD supervision. The main takeaway is that standard metrics and single judges correlate poorly with human judgments on realistic dialogue, especially for long-range conversational properties, while a simple learned ensemble tracks humans better. The interesting contribution is less the ensemble itself and more the empirical demonstration that synthetic-data-based judge validation does not transfer cleanly to real human-scale conversations.2a:T46f,The most important results are about dataset scale, distribution, and metric validity. UPHELD contains hundreds of complete dialogues, more than 30,000 human-written turns, and 153,578 dense turn-level human labels. The authors position this as roughly an order-of-magnitude increase over comparable human-verified dialogue evaluation resources. They also analyze dialogue length distributions and argue that realistic human task-oriented conversations are usually much shorter than many benchmark setups; they cite an average of about 9.3 turns in fully human-collected datasets, with Topical-Chat maxing out at 21 turns, and allow writers to stop naturally rather than forcing long transcripts. On evaluation, traditional NLP metrics and single LLM judges show weak correlation with human ratings on UPHELD, especially on style and content consistency, while the learned mixture-of-judges improves correlation. The excerpt does not expose the full numeric table, but the paper’s claim is that the gains are consistent across settings. The error analysis also emphasizes failures caused by synthetic dialogue bias and judge leakage.2b:T5d4,MemToC is built as a controlled post-tool-call benchmark derived from ToolHop.
- Data pipeline: start from ToolHop sub-questions with locally executable tool calls and verified intermediate answers; keep only self-contained facts with short verifiable answers.
- Pre-construct benchmark artifacts before evaluating any model: question q, verified gold g, executable call, and curated distractors to synthesize plausible incorrect tool returns. Two annotators independently review for self-containedness, answer leakage, answer validity, and distractor plausibility.
- For each model and each prompt wording variant, first elicit a closed-book answer m (forced to answer, no abstention) and score it against g to determine whether the model’s memory is correct.
- Then present the same question with an injected tool return r under four conditions: correct return, incorrect return (built from verified answers and human-validated distractors), error payload, and no return. Because r is injected, tool correctness is known a priori.
- This yields a source-correctness matrix where the “correct behavior” is well-defined (use correct tools, resist incorrect tools), with tool-error/no-tool as controls.
- Evaluation details include extraction-first answer matching and three instruction-wording variants with fixed question text.
- Mitigations: compare prompting vs SFT vs DPO, using cross-fitting over ToolHop chains so that each reported fine-tuned prediction is on held-out questions.2c:T525,The benchmark is organized around two aligned axes. On the math side, the authors define a taxonomy of atomic capabilities in three levels: Level 1 covers foundational concepts and calculation, including symbol recognition, concept understanding, and calculation; Level 2 covers advanced reasoning and application, including spatial perception, formalization, deductive and inductive reasoning, and mathematical modeling; Level 3 covers meta-cognition, including theorem application, self-reflection, and new knowledge learning. On the agent side, these are aligned with Planning, Action, and Feedback. The key point is that the benchmark evaluates the intersection rather than simply relabeling math skills: planning is framed as capability selection, ordering, and next-step decision making; action as isolated execution of a single atomic capability; feedback as monitoring and revising an existing trajectory. The dataset construction uses an automated pipeline with data collection, trajectory synthesis, multi-stage filtering, and fine-grained annotation, and it includes both text-only and multimodal settings. A notable design choice is to decouple intrinsic agentic evaluation from full agent deployment, so the benchmark measures reusable process capabilities without requiring a complete tool-using system.2d:T4e0,1) Task formulation (VTEC): split into Error Detection (locate wrong tokens/regions) and Error Correction (produce the corrected text), with samples designed to be unsolvable from linguistic context alone.
2) High-quality test set: manually curated real-world images with natural errors; strict selection rule that visual cues are necessary; multi-stage annotation with cross-checking and adjudication; plus error-free images from EST-VQA/TextVQA for detection calibration. Final test set size: 1,229.
3) Scalable training set for targeted training/RL: a three-stage Generate–Edit–Examination pipeline.
- Generate: GPT-4o proposes corrupted text; Qwen3-235B filters out cases fixable by language-only reasoning.
- Edit: Qwen-Image-Edit-2509 writes the corrupted text back into the image regions.
- Examination: Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B-Instruct performs correction/verification to filter low-quality edits and label mismatches.
This yields 4,531 synthetic-but-checked training samples.
4) Evaluation paradigms: (a) prompt-based strategies (direct correction, background information enhancement), and (b) targeted model training including RL-based tuning, aiming to approximate each model’s ceiling before concluding there remains a large human gap.2e:T5eb,FaithSieve has three stages: localization, verification, and synthesis. In localization, the system decomposes coarse benchmark steps into finer EdgeUnits using a proof-state-tree representation. Each transition in this tree corresponds to a local reasoning responsibility, which makes the evaluation target much sharper than judging an entire paragraph-like step. In verification, the method extracts typed proof obligations from suspicious transitions. These obligations are not treated uniformly; they encode different kinds of reasoning responsibilities such as algebraic transformation, implication transfer, existence construction, boundary-condition handling, or case transitions. The system then gathers evidence through both lightweight checks and Lean-based validation. Lean is used as a structured evidence source rather than a direct judge. A key safeguard is the semantic alignment score, which measures whether the formalized statement preserves the assumptions, directionality, quantification, and strength of the original informal obligation. Lean evidence is only trusted when this alignment is high enough, preventing drifted or weakened targets from producing false reassurance. In synthesis, local evidence is aggregated back to the original step level to predict the first erroneous benchmark step. The main novelty is the full pipeline: decomposition into local transitions, typed obligations, formal checking gated by semantic alignment, and evidence aggregation for first-error localization.2f:T506,The paper introduces two expert-verified datasets: ProofLoc-Olympiad with 350 algebra and number theory problems, and ProofLoc-University with 200 advanced mathematics problems. On ProofLoc-Olympiad, FaithSieve reaches 81.43% exact first-error accuracy, compared with 72.29% for a GPT-5.4 direct-judging baseline, an absolute gain of 9.14 points. The provided text also states that FaithSieve shows clear gains on ProofLoc-University, although the exact number is not visible in the excerpt. The evaluation follows the standard first-error localization setup, reporting both binary accuracy and exact accuracy, with the latter requiring the correct first-error index. The experimental story is important because it tests whether local formal evidence, when filtered for semantic faithfulness, actually improves early-error localization. The likely key ablations are removing EdgeUnit decomposition, which should make local flaws harder to isolate; removing semantic alignment gating, which should increase false positives from target drift; and relying only on natural-language judging, which should weaken precise localization. Together these results support a narrow but important claim: formal verification helps only when the verified target is both local and semantically faithful.30:T4b2,Vision-Free Adaptation (VFA) reframes fine-tuning as a task vector and composes task vectors over a shared backbone.
- Let the base text LLM be θ_base. Fine-tune θ_base on multilingual text D_multi to obtain θ_FT. Define the multilingual task vector as Δ_multi = θ_FT − θ_base.
- Given an existing MLLM with backbone parameters θ_MLLM (already vision-aligned), VFA avoids updating θ_MLLM with text-only gradients. Instead it merges the multilingual delta into the MLLM: θ_merged = θ_MLLM + α·Δ_multi, where α controls the trade-off between multilingual gains and preserving multimodal behavior.
- Vision encoder and projection modules are frozen to prevent drifting of cross-modal mapping without visual supervision.
- The paper discusses multiple merging operators (explicitly including weight averaging / linear interpolation) to navigate the stability–plasticity trade-off.

Rationale: direct text-only tuning on θ_MLLM perturbs shared backbone representations that also support vision grounding; learning Δ_multi in the θ_base coordinate system and injecting it as a controlled increment reduces destructive interference and enables reuse across multiple MLLMs sharing θ_base.31:T8d1,全文的核心思路是把重复退化看成一个离散动力系统中的“入环”和“困环”问题，而不是把所有重复都归因于单步概率偏差。作者先用 coverage-based 的视角刻画 repetition loop：生成序列中往往存在一个短 N-gram 循环，覆盖了后续大部分 token。基于这个观察，他们把退化拆成两部分。

第一是 loop entry risk，也就是模型在某个关键上下文第一次踏入重复轨道的概率。作者发现这通常是一个 sharp entry event：只要在入环点替换掉少数几个高概率 token，后续轨迹就可能完全摆脱重复。第二是 loop persistence，即一旦进入循环，模型在连续多个时间步里持续留在循环中的倾向。作者指出 persistence 才是长程重复的主导项，因为它会在每一步累乘放大。

在这个分析上，论文进一步解释了为什么剪枝和朴素蒸馏会恶化问题。剪枝后的学生模型容量更紧，若仍用标准 distillation，它容易把概率质量泄漏到 teacher 原本压低的 token 上，这会提高 entry risk；同时它还会压扁 nucleus 内几个接近并列的合理候选，使模型更早承诺到单一路径，降低 escape mass，从而提高 persistence。这里的 escape mass 指的是在当前采样集合内，分配给“可接受但不重复”的替代 token 的总概率质量。

基于这两个失真来源，作者提出两个训练目标。FOCUS 用于抑制 leakage。它不是均匀模仿 teacher 全分布，而是对高置信 teacher 区域加权更高，让学生优先学稳 teacher 明确支持的 token，减少容量不足时向低支持区域漂移。RePAIR 用于降低 persistence。它围绕退化 onset 构造 continuation pair：一个是实际走向重复的 negative continuation，另一个是从同一起点重新生成得到的 non-degenerate continuation。然后用 margin-based objective 提升 onset 附近那些合理替代 token 的相对得分，增加 escape mass，避免模型过早锁死在循环轨道上。

从方法定位看，FOCUS 更像是改造蒸馏损失，RePAIR 更像是针对 loop-sensitive context 的对比式修复。两者互补：前者减少“误入”，后者增加“脱困”能力。32:T96f,The central idea is to treat repetition as a dynamical process with two stages: entering a loop and staying in it. The paper adopts a coverage-based view of repetition loops, where a short recurring n-gram accounts for a large fraction of the generated continuation. Based on this, degeneration is decomposed into two quantities.

The first is loop entry risk: the probability that generation first falls into a repetitive trajectory at a sensitive context. The authors argue that this is often a sharp event. Replacing only a few high-probability tokens near the onset can be enough to divert the trajectory away from repetition. The second is loop persistence: once the model has entered the loop, how likely it is to remain there over successive decoding steps. Persistence is the dominant factor for long-horizon degeneration because it compounds across in-loop transitions.

This decomposition is then used to explain why pruning and naive distillation make the problem worse. Under reduced capacity, standard distillation can leak probability mass into tokens that the teacher had strongly suppressed, which raises entry risk. At the same time, it can collapse several plausible near-tie alternatives inside the nucleus set, causing the model to commit too early to a single path and reducing the escape mass available to non-repetitive continuations. Here, escape mass refers to the total probability assigned to acceptable alternative tokens that would let decoding leave the repetitive trajectory.

The paper proposes two complementary training objectives. FOCUS addresses leakage. Instead of matching the teacher distribution uniformly, it reweights distillation toward high-confidence teacher regions so that the student prioritizes tokens the teacher clearly supports, reducing drift into low-support regions. RePAIR addresses persistence. Around detected degeneration onsets, it constructs paired continuations: a repetitive negative continuation and a regenerated non-degenerate continuation from the same onset. A margin-based objective then increases the relative score of plausible alternatives near the onset, enlarging escape mass and reducing early commitment to the loop.

Conceptually, FOCUS is a distillation correction objective, while RePAIR is a contrastive repair objective for loop-sensitive contexts. They target different parts of the failure mode and are designed to be complementary.33:T6ba,Experiments are conducted in a post-pruning pipeline on Llama-family models, with LoRA-based finetuning after pruning. Evaluation covers both open-ended continuation on WikiText-103 and instruction-style generation on Self-Instruct. Importantly, the paper does not stop at perplexity. It also reports generation-quality and degeneration-sensitive metrics such as MAUVE, CREP, EAD, and semantic similarity measures including BERTScore.

The main empirical result is consistent: FOCUS and RePAIR reduce repetition and improve distributional and semantic quality, while incurring only a small increase in perplexity. The paper contrasts this with standard anti-repetition decoding heuristics, which can suppress repetition but often damage the language-model distribution and hurt coherence. The advantage here is that the method does not blindly penalize previously generated tokens; it selectively reshapes probability allocation at loop-sensitive contexts.

The most useful ablation-level insights are threefold. First, long-run degeneration is driven more by persistence than by the one-time entry event. Second, under nucleus decoding, preserving probability mass on several plausible alternatives is critical for avoiding lock-in to a repetitive trajectory. Third, FOCUS is compatible with existing training-based mitigation objectives and still improves generation quality when combined, suggesting that it addresses a relatively distinct source of distortion.

The abstract does not expose all numeric details, and the paper emphasizes consistency of trends more than a single headline number. When reading the results, the most informative view is the joint movement of repetition metrics, MAUVE/EAD, and perplexity.34:T488,A good reading order is: failure-mode decomposition, objective design, then empirical validation. Start with the introduction and the first half of the method section to understand why the authors separate degeneration into entry risk and persistence, and why escape mass matters. Then read the definitions of FOCUS and RePAIR carefully, paying attention to which distortion each one is meant to correct. Finally, inspect the main tables on WikiText-103 and Self-Instruct to see whether repetition reduction comes without a large loss in language quality.

If you only have 25 to 35 minutes, focus on four parts: the dynamical analysis of repetition loops, the reweighting logic in FOCUS, the paired-continuation construction in RePAIR, and the comparison against standard repetition-penalty style baselines in terms of perplexity and generation quality. For a deeper read, two follow-up questions are worth thinking about: whether this token-level guidance transfers to distillation, quantization recovery, or expert pruning in MoE models, and whether loop-sensitive contexts could be surfaced earlier as curriculum or hard-example signals during pretraining.35:T4d0,The method builds on Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. The student is initialized from the domain-specialized model. A frozen copy of that model serves as the domain teacher, while the original open general model serves as the general teacher. The student samples trajectories on domain and general prompts, and the corresponding teacher evaluates each sampled token under the same prefix. The key quantity is the distillation advantage, defined as teacher log probability minus student log probability. Positive advantage increases the sampled token probability; negative advantage decreases it. The paper argues that standard MOPD suffers from two issues. First, ordinary on-policy sampling rarely exposes tokens with large positive teacher-student gaps, because samples come from the student distribution. Second, the advantage sign alone does not tell whether the update is safe. The proposed uncertainty-calibrated MOPD addresses these issues with dual-temperature sampling to broaden candidate trajectories, positive-advantage-density filtering to keep rollouts rich in high-gain tokens, and teacher-endorsement calibration to validate whether reinforcement or suppression is supported by the teacher’s own distribution.36:T50a,The paper first measures how instruction quality shapes the response distribution using Best-of-N and Worst-of-N analyses. It takes 1.5K UltraFeedback instructions, partitions them into High, Mid, and Low quality groups using rubric-based instruction scores, samples multiple responses with LLaMA3-8B, and scores them with a reward model. Best-of-N estimates the response-quality ceiling under a fixed sampling budget; Worst-of-N estimates the floor. High-quality instructions produce higher rewards on both sides, indicating that instruction quality affects not only the best available candidate but also the prevalence of trivial failures. The paper then relates this to DPO gradients: informative preference learning requires useful margins between chosen and rejected responses. Poor instructions can yield weak or trivial pairs, reducing the effective training signal. The proposed pipeline selects weak instructions using reward signals and revises them with rubric-guided LLM feedback. The revision is diagnostic rather than generic rewriting: the rubric targets ambiguity, missing constraints, underspecification, and comparability issues so that subsequent candidate responses become more informative. The pipeline keeps the examples instead of discarding low-quality instructions.37:T40c,SWE-Prime uses a two-stage, multi-granularity selection pipeline. Stage 1 operates at the trajectory level and keeps successful traces that are good in process quality, result quality, and representativeness. Process quality is measured from interaction logs using four signals: whether the trace follows an observe-edit-verify workflow, whether tool calls are reliable, whether there is continuous redundancy, and whether the trace uses Git-history leakage or similar shortcuts. Result quality favors minimal and sufficient patches. Representativeness prevents the selected set from collapsing to a narrow task type. Stage 2 operates at the semantic-segment level. Instead of judging isolated tool steps, it groups action-observation spans into behaviorally coherent segments and selects the segments that actually contribute to the final fix. During SFT, the full trajectory is kept as context, but only selected segments contribute to the loss. This avoids losing context while preventing low-value behavior from producing gradients.38:T404,Boundary-Calibrated Intervention Transfer, or BCIT, stores each update as a state-bound record: the source context, the observed source evidence, prespecified applicability conditions and named hard conflicts, and provenance links for the model, data, configuration, and evaluation artifacts. The decision is made for a pair consisting of one candidate and the current context. A non-compensable conflict leads to rejection. Insufficient or unresolved evidence triggers a bounded validation run from the current parent. Only candidates satisfying a frozen authorization rule receive the full training budget. A fully trained child is still subject to a separate promote-or-rollback rule. The system may generate a small number of new atomic proposals when progress stalls, but those proposals have no fabricated source evidence and cannot skip validation. The main distinction from simple experience replay is that an update is not labeled globally as good or bad; its evidence remains tied to the context where it was observed.39:T841,技术上分成两个层次。第一层是 PrivBoN。标准 BoN 是从基模型采样 n 个候选，用奖励模型打分后取 argmax。作者在每个候选分数上加入独立 Gumbel 噪声，尺度设为 sigma=2Δr/ε，其中 Δr 是奖励模型对相邻偏好数据集的敏感度。这样做有两个效果：从隐私角度，它等价于指数机制，直接给出 ε-DP；从优化角度，带 Gumbel 噪声的 argmax 在大样本极限下对应 softmax 选择，也就是求解一个 KL 正则化的最优策略。论文进一步把这个 regularization 强度与 Huang 等人关于 BoN regret skyline 的理论接起来，证明当隐私预算超过阈值 ε*(x) 时，DP 所要求的噪声强度正好落在 regret 最优区间，因此不会额外损伤对齐。

第二层是 PrivITP。PrivBoN 的问题在于“隐私免费”的阈值依赖未知的 coverage coefficient C^{π*}，实际系统无法验证。为此作者提出 private inference-time pessimism：先用 χ² 正则化的 rejection sampling 构造更保守的选择分布，再用两阶段 Gaussian 机制实现近似 DP。这里的关键不是简单加高斯噪声，而是把基于 ReLU 的拒绝采样重写成 randomized-threshold mechanism，从而得到一个与候选数 n 无关、只依赖实际 halting time 的 ex-post DP 保证。理论分析里还有一个重要工具：smoothed-weight policy comparison lemma，用策略权重函数的 ℓ∞ 距离控制 value gap，于是 regret 可以分解成奖励模型误差项和隐私噪声项，形式上是 O(sqrt(C^{π*}(ε_RM^2 + R_max σ)))。这比直接分析 noisy argmax 更细，因为它把 privacy cost 单独拆了出来，也解释了为什么 PrivITP 在未知 coverage 情况下更稳。

从方法取舍看，作者没有走训练时 DP-RM 或 DP-RLHF 的路线，因为目标是冻结模型、服务时对齐；也没有只做 heuristic smoothing，因为那样没有正式隐私保证。选择 Gumbel/指数机制和 Gaussian 两阶段机制，都是为了把 selection rule 写成可证明的随机机制，而不是把隐私当作附带噪声工程。3a:T87e,The method has two layers.

The first is Private Best-of-N (PrivBoN). Standard BoN samples n candidates from a base policy, scores them with a reward model, and returns the argmax. PrivBoN adds i.i.d. Gumbel noise to each reward score with scale sigma = 2Δr/ε, where Δr is the reward-model sensitivity under neighboring preference datasets. This gives ε-differential privacy because noisy argmax with Gumbel noise is equivalent to the exponential mechanism. More interestingly, in the large-n limit the same mechanism implements a KL-regularized alignment optimum: the induced selection rule becomes a softmax policy over rewards. The paper then connects this regularization strength to the regret skyline from prior BoN theory, showing that when the privacy budget exceeds a critical threshold ε*(x), the privacy-imposed noise is exactly in the regret-optimal range. In that regime, privacy is effectively free from an alignment-regret perspective.

The second is Private Inference-Time Pessimism (PrivITP). PrivBoN depends on an unknown coverage coefficient C^{π*}, so the favorable threshold cannot be verified in practice. PrivITP addresses this by combining χ²-regularized rejection sampling with a two-phase Gaussian mechanism. The key move is not simply adding Gaussian noise, but reformulating a ReLU-based rejection sampler as a randomized-threshold mechanism. That yields an ex-post approximate-DP guarantee whose bound is independent of the candidate count n and depends only on the realized halting time. The regret analysis uses a smoothed-weight policy comparison lemma that controls value gaps through the ℓ∞ distance between policy weight functions, leading to a bound of the form O(sqrt(C^{π*}(ε_RM^2 + R_max σ))). This cleanly separates reward-model error from privacy cost and explains why PrivITP is more robust when coverage is unknown.

A notable design choice is that the paper does not solve the problem by retraining the reward model with DP or by using heuristic smoothing. It treats inference-time selection itself as the object of study and chooses mechanisms whose stochasticity is analytically tied to both privacy and alignment.3b:T5b6,The empirical study spans four reward models, two language models of different sizes, and three benchmark families: GSM8K, MMLU, and MATH. The main findings are consistent with the theory.

1. Standard BoN is not scaling-monotonic under reward-model error. As N increases, performance can peak and then degrade, which is the practical signature of reward hacking.
2. PrivBoN mitigates this failure mode. With theoretically calibrated Gumbel noise, the performance curve as a function of N becomes much more stable, and the method preserves the benefit of test-time compute without over-amplifying reward-model error.
3. PrivITP consistently matches or outperforms PrivBoN at comparable privacy levels, especially in regimes where large candidate sets make BoN more brittle.
4. In multi-query deployment, the ex-post composition structure matters. When combined with the FSRC accounting framework, PrivITP can answer substantially more queries under a fixed privacy budget than standard worst-case composition.

The most informative part of the experiments is not any single headline number, but the shape of the scaling curves: whether BoN degrades at large N, whether the private mechanisms restore monotonicity or at least suppress the pathology, and whether the observed privacy-performance tradeoff matches the threshold story from the theory. If the full paper provides tables with ε, N, query counts, and task metrics, those are the right axes to inspect.3c:T527,Start with Sections 3 and 4, then return to the introduction once the mechanisms are clear. Four parts deserve attention.

First, read the equivalence between Gumbel-noisy argmax and the exponential mechanism. That is the bridge that makes PrivBoN simultaneously a DP mechanism and a KL-regularized selector.

Second, inspect the derivation and meaning of the critical threshold ε*(x). This is where the paper ties privacy, reward-model error, and coverage together, and it also reveals why PrivBoN alone is not operationally sufficient.

Third, read the reformulation lemma and the ex-post DP proof for PrivITP carefully. If you work on adaptive compute, test-time search, or verifier-guided reranking, this part is likely the most reusable.

Fourth, focus on the scaling plots and the composition experiments rather than only final averages. The central empirical question is whether the private mechanisms prevent the large-N failure mode and how much budget is saved in repeated-query settings.

Although tagged as skim, the theory sections are worth a deeper read if your interests include reward-model serving or the interface between post-training and deployment. A fast pass through the introduction, core theorems, and figures should take about 45 minutes; a full technical read is closer to 1.5 to 2.5 hours.3d:T864,方法上，这不是提出全新 ES 变体，而是对 ES 作为 reasoning post-training 工具做系统分析，并与 GRPO 做机制层面对照。ES 的基本过程是：对当前参数加随机扰动，得到一组 perturbed policies；每个扰动模型只做前向采样和 verifier 打分；再把 reward-weighted perturbations 聚合成更新方向。它绕开反向传播，因此显存占用低、并行性强。GRPO 则是在单一策略上采样多条响应，用组内相对优势构造 token-level policy gradient 更新。

作者的理论部分聚焦于“为什么 ES 可能有更高 Pass@K”。核心概念是 verifier-projected Jensen-Shannon diversity：不是看表面文本多样性，而是看 ES 群体在 verifier 视角下覆盖了多少不同的有效推理模式。论文论证这种群体多样性有助于 repeated sampling success，并且可以转化为更新后策略更高的 Pass@K。这个角度很关键，因为它把 ES 的优势从“无梯度优化也能训”转成“参数空间探索保留了解空间覆盖”。

经验分析部分主要有三块。第一，作者比较 ES 与 GRPO 的熵动态和 reasoning coverage，指出 GRPO 会出现 entropy collapse，而 ES 没有同样程度的收缩。第二，作者分析 ES 的参数变化分布，发现真正与任务相关的效果集中在少量较大幅度更新上，而大量参数变化在 perturbation cancellation 后功能贡献很小，因此 whole-model drift 不能直接等价为 widespread functional change。第三，作者系统扫超参数和估计器设计，结论包括：z-score reward normalization 是 ES 成功训练的关键；在 reasoning 的离散 reward 场景下，zeroth-order SFT 常用的 two-point estimator 并不带来优势；随着 pretrained model 规模增大，达到有效优化所需的 population size 反而下降。

论文还提出两个顺序组合方案 ES→GRPO 和 GRPO→ES，用来结合 GRPO 在 Pass@1 上的优势与 ES 在 Pass@K 上的优势。这不是特别复杂的算法创新，但很实用，因为它把两种更新机制看成对策略分布施加不同偏置的阶段性工具。3e:T9c4,This is not primarily a new ES algorithm; it is a systematic mechanistic study of ES as a reasoning post-training paradigm, with GRPO as the main gradient-based comparator. In ES, the current parameters are perturbed to form a population of policies, each perturbed model is evaluated by forward-only sampling and verifier rewards, and reward-weighted perturbations are aggregated into an update estimate. This avoids backpropagation, making ES memory-efficient and easy to parallelize. GRPO, in contrast, samples multiple responses from a single policy and applies a token-level policy-gradient-style update based on relative advantages within the group.

The theoretical contribution centers on why ES may achieve higher Pass@K. The paper introduces verifier-projected Jensen-Shannon diversity, which measures diversity not at the surface-text level but in terms of distinct reasoning modes that matter under the verifier. The argument is that higher diversity across the ES population increases repeated-sampling success and can translate into higher Pass@K in the updated policy. This is an important framing shift: the advantage of ES is not just that it is zeroth-order, but that population-based parameter-space exploration preserves broader solution coverage.

Empirically, the paper analyzes three aspects. First, it compares entropy dynamics and reasoning coverage under ES and GRPO, showing that GRPO exhibits entropy collapse while ES does not collapse in the same way. Second, it studies the distribution of parameter changes under ES and finds that task-relevant effects are concentrated in a small subset of larger-magnitude updates, while much of the overall drift contributes little after perturbation cancellation. This supports the claim that whole-model drift is not the same as widespread functional change. Third, it performs a systematic sweep over hyperparameters and estimator variants, finding that z-score reward normalization is critical, that the two-point estimator commonly used in zeroth-order SFT offers no benefit under discrete reasoning rewards, and that the population size needed for effective optimization decreases as pretrained model scale increases.

The paper also proposes two sequential compositions, ES→GRPO and GRPO→ES, to combine GRPO’s strength on Pass@1 with ES’s strength on Pass@K. The idea is simple but useful: the two methods impose different biases on the policy distribution and can be staged rather than treated as mutually exclusive.3f:T58b,From the full text provided, the main experiments are conducted on GSM8K and DeepScaleR, comparing ES against GRPO on Pass@1, Pass@K, entropy dynamics, capability retention, and hyperparameter sensitivity. The main empirical result is that ES improves Pass@1 while achieving higher Pass@K than GRPO, and it does so without the same degree of entropy collapse. In effect, GRPO tends to sharpen the policy around a smaller set of successful reasoning trajectories, whereas ES preserves a broader set of viable paths.

On capability preservation, the paper reports that under appropriate training settings, held-out capabilities remain largely intact. Combined with the parameter-change analysis, this supports the claim that previously observed forgetting under ES is better explained by training-set overfitting than by parameter drift per se.

The hyperparameter study yields several practical takeaways: z-score reward normalization is a key ingredient for successful ES training; the two-point estimator does not help in reasoning tasks with discrete verifier rewards; and larger pretrained models require smaller population sizes for effective ES optimization. The excerpt does not include the full numeric tables, so the exact margins should be checked in the main results section, especially the absolute Pass@1/Pass@K gaps, entropy trajectories, and the magnitude of degradation on held-out capability benchmarks.40:T417,A good way to read it is by following the three research questions. Start with the introduction and the RQ1-RQ3 framing so the paper is read as a study of optimization behavior rather than a simple ES-versus-GRPO leaderboard comparison. Then read the theory section on verifier-projected JS diversity to understand why the authors attribute Pass@K gains to broader reasoning coverage rather than incidental sampling effects. After that, focus on three groups of experiments: Pass@1/Pass@K with entropy dynamics, parameter-drift versus capability-retention analysis, and the hyperparameter/estimator ablations.

If the goal is triage, 25-35 minutes should be enough. Prioritize the main ES-vs-GRPO plots, the evidence for entropy collapse, the ablation on z-score normalization, and the sequential composition results. For deeper reading, inspect how reasoning coverage is operationalized, which held-out capabilities are used, and whether the claim that required population size decreases with model scale holds consistently across model families.41:T91c,RubricRM is a pairwise generative reward model. The input is an instruction q and two candidate images I_A and I_B; image editing additionally includes a source image. Instead of directly predicting which image is better, the model generates a structured rubric and dimension-level assessments for both candidates in one pass. The rubric includes task intent, atomic evaluation dimensions d_i, weights w_i over those dimensions, and scoring criteria L_i for each dimension. The model then assigns per-dimension scores s_i^k to each image, aggregates them with the learned weights, and derives the final preference from the weighted totals.

The key design choice is not merely to produce free-form rationales, but to turn explanation into a structured scoring protocol. That makes the output more controllable and allows training signals to operate at the dimension level rather than only at the final pairwise label.

Training has two stages. In the SFT stage, the model learns rubric trajectories synthesized by a teacher: how to decompose the instruction into intent, dimensions, weights, criteria, and then score the candidates. In the RL stage, the paper uses dimension-level GRPO. The objective aligns predicted score differences with reference score differences on each rubric dimension, and penalizes preference-direction reversals more strongly. This indicates that the authors care not only about final pairwise accuracy but also about the internal correctness of the dimension-wise judgment process.

Data construction is a major part of the contribution. The paper argues that dynamic rubric learning requires broad instruction coverage; otherwise the model will learn biased rubric templates and generalize poorly. To address this, the authors aggregate multiple open-source preference datasets, analyze instruction types for text-to-image generation and image editing, define classification criteria, and use DeepSeek-V3.2 for automatic taxonomy annotation. They then perform balanced sampling, identify underrepresented categories, synthesize additional prompts for those categories, generate candidate images, collect expert preference annotations, and finally synthesize rubric trajectories for each preference sample. The resulting training sets contain 31.8K text-to-image samples and 30.7K image-editing samples.42:T54f,The experiments train dedicated models for text-to-image generation and image editing on Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3.5-9B backbones. The headline numbers reported in the text are: RubricRM-Gen-9B reaches 72.00 on MMRB2, 74.12 on GenAI-Bench, and 84.45 on GenAI-Bench-Verified; RubricRM-Edit-9B reaches 75.40 on MMRB2, 46.40 on EditReward-ERB, and 85.64 on EditScore-ERB. The paper states that these results outperform existing reward-model baselines across both generation and editing.

The most informative parts of the experimental section are likely not just the main table, but three kinds of analysis: (1) the effect of the two-stage pipeline—SFT only, preference optimization only, and SFT plus GRPO; (2) the effect of data coverage—whether balanced sampling and long-tail supplementation improve generalization on underrepresented instruction types; and (3) the value of the rubric structure itself—how much is lost when replacing dynamic rubrics with fixed criteria or a plain scalar preference head.

The provided excerpt does not include the full ablation tables or per-baseline gains, so it would be unsafe to fabricate exact margins. Still, the reported numbers already show two useful facts: the method works on both generation and editing, and the reward model is trained as a generative multimodal model rather than a conventional scalar scorer.43:T496,This is a skim paper. The main value is in the task formulation, data design, and training decomposition rather than mathematical novelty. Start with the introduction to see that the proposal is not just an explainable reward model, but a model that first generates a rubric and then scores under that rubric. Then read the task definition carefully to understand the four rubric components and how final preference is aggregated from dimension-level scores. After that, focus on the two-stage training setup: what SFT teaches, and what GRPO corrects. Finally, read the data section, especially the instruction taxonomy, balanced sampling, and long-tail supplementation pipeline, because dynamic rubrics only make sense if the training distribution is broad enough.

A practical reading order is: task examples/cases → rubric structure → SFT objective → RL objective → data construction → main results → ablations. Around 20–35 minutes should be enough for the main ideas. If deciding whether to follow up, check two things first: the gain over fixed-dimension reward models, and whether dimension-level RL actually improves final pairwise preference accuracy.44:T4ed,The main controlled experiments use the strictly monolingual Goldfish causal LM families. The 5MB and 10MB data tiers use 39M-parameter models, while the 100MB and 1000MB tiers use 125M-parameter models. The models have disjoint vocabularies, parameters, and corpora. The authors also replicate the effect on five independently developed monolingual models around 1B parameters. A GlotLID-v3 audit reports at most about 0.1% English content in the non-English corpora used in the main analysis. Evaluation uses FLORES-200, Tatoeba, OPUS, and BouQUET over 36 language pairs. Matched parallel sentences consistently show higher CKA than shuffled controls across layers and language pairs. On FLORES with SGPT pooling, last-layer matched CKA averages about 0.78, versus about 0.17 for the shuffled baseline. Alignment becomes stronger with larger data, larger models, and closer languages. A Procrustes rotation is sufficient for useful alignment; affine and MLP mappings lower reconstruction error but hurt retrieval. In causal patching, rotated English residuals often flip a target-language model’s factual cloze prediction to the donor capital, with the effect holding across five unrelated target languages and approaching the within-model patching ceiling.45:T7b8,SCIT，Suffix Cache Interchange Test，本质上是一个 cache-level counterfactual intervention protocol。给定 recipient 问题 r 和 source 问题 s，先构造一个精确可验证的反事实：source 提供某个中间变量的替代值，把它与 recipient 其余变量组合后，得到已知正确的 counterfactual answer。然后让 latent-CoT 模型分别在 r 和 s 上 rollout 到某个 latent step t，在这个 patch 点把 source 的某个缓存切片插入 recipient，对比 recipient 的答案是否朝 source 定义的反事实移动。

方法不是只做一次 patch，而是做一整套 intervention grid。维度包括：patch 哪个 cache segment；只换 K、只换 V、还是 K/V 一起换；是否同时复制当前 hidden state；是否使用语义相近但答案相同的 source control；是否做 decoded validation，确认被 patch 的 latent 轨迹确实对应可读出的反事实行为；以及 matched corruption，用同一区域做破坏性干预来测试 necessity。作者据此定义三个状态：active carrier，指最小且 gated 的 cache segment，在 oracle interchange 下足够，并且主要竞争解释无法匹配；carrier shift，指存在可转移效应，但活跃片段与主结论模型不同；no-call，指由于能力、支持度、decoded 行为或控制检查不过关，不能下机制判断。

从全文看，论文最重要的技术细节有三点。第一，它强调 exact source-recipient counterfactual，而不是开放式 prompt patching，这样可以避免目标答案不明确。第二，它把 sufficiency 和 necessity 分开，且 necessity 通过 matched corruption 而不是随意噪声注入来做，减少“哪里都能破坏”的伪结论。第三，它把 hidden state 与 cache object 明确区分：h_t 不是 cache 的一部分，只有显式复制时才算干预对象。这让“是当前状态在算，还是历史 value 轨迹在承载”这个问题可以被单独检验。46:T74d,SCIT, the Suffix Cache Interchange Test, is a cache-level counterfactual intervention protocol. For a recipient problem r and a source problem s, the method first constructs an exact, verifiable counterfactual: the source supplies a changed intermediate quantity, which is then combined with the recipient’s remaining variables to define a known counterfactual answer. Both source and recipient are rolled out to a latent step t. At that patch point, a selected cache slice from the source is transplanted into the recipient, optionally together with the current hidden state, and the test checks whether the recipient moves toward the source-defined counterfactual answer.

The protocol is deliberately grid-like rather than a single patch experiment. It varies: which cache segment is patched; whether only K, only V, or both are copied; whether the hidden state is also copied; whether semantic source controls with the same answer are used; whether decoded validation confirms that the patched latent trajectory corresponds to the intended counterfactual behavior; and whether matched corruption on the same region supports necessity. Based on this battery, the paper defines three outcomes: active carrier, carrier shift, and no-call.

Three technical choices are especially important. First, the paper uses exact source-recipient counterfactuals rather than open-ended prompt patching, which keeps the target answer unambiguous. Second, it separates sufficiency from necessity, and tests necessity with matched corruption rather than arbitrary noise, reducing the risk of overcalling mechanisms. Third, it explicitly distinguishes the current hidden state from the cache object: h_t is not part of the cache unless copied by intervention. That makes it possible to ask whether the computation is carried by the present state or by a historical value trajectory.47:T6f0,The experiments are centered on controlled arithmetic settings, chosen because they allow exact counterfactual labels and clean negative controls. The main result is checkpoint-specific but strong: on the CODI-GPT2 arithmetic checkpoint, counterfactual answer transfer occurs primarily through value-cache suffix trajectories, with the strongest restricted effect in middle-to-late value segments. This region is not only sufficient under oracle interchange, but also supported by matched-corruption necessity, which is why the authors make a full mechanism call for a late-value-suffix carrier. In contrast, hidden-only transfer, key-only transfer, single-token triggers, same-answer semantic source controls, and partial-variable controls are all much weaker and do not explain the same effect.

On a Sim-CoT-style GPT-2 reproduction, the pattern is directionally similar: value-suffix sufficiency and decoded transfer are present, but matched-corruption necessity is not strong enough for a full necessity claim. The paper is explicit about that limitation.

A broader checkpoint sweep shows that the mechanism is not uniform. Some arithmetic-like GPT-2/1B cells preserve latent-tail value/KV transfer, while several competent 8B or repaired non-arithmetic cells shift the carrier to prompt-prefix or full-cache K/V. The authors call this a carrier shift rather than a replication of the CODI-GPT2 mechanism. Some cells are labeled no-call because competence gating, decoded behavior, support, or source-control checks fail. This is one of the paper’s strengths: it does not argue that latent CoT universally lives in value-cache suffixes; it provides a framework that can separate local mechanism evidence, regime shifts, and cases where no mechanism claim is justified.48:T4bf,For a skim, focus on the protocol before the results. The introduction already states the scope clearly: the goal is not to prove a universal latent-CoT mechanism, but to define a carrier-test contract. Start with Figure 1 and the method section to understand the four-step flow: define the patch object, specify the intervention grid, validate the readout, and apply the carrier decision rule.

Then read how the arithmetic counterfactuals are constructed. That part explains why the source-recipient pairing is exact and why the protocol is not yet directly portable to open-domain reasoning tasks.

In the experiments, prioritize the CODI-GPT2 main result, because that is the only place where the paper claims a full sufficiency-plus-necessity mechanism. Then read the Sim-CoT-style checkpoint to see why it is treated as partial corroboration rather than full replication. Finally, inspect the carrier-regime map and pay attention to the gating logic behind active carrier, carrier shift, and no-call.

A 30-minute read is enough to capture the main idea. For a deeper pass, the appendix matters: intervention tables, prompt templates, and the full evaluation battery are where the paper earns its credibility.49:T929,方法核心是一个 iterative prune-heal-backtrack 流程。模型被表示为 residual-stream writers/readers 构成的有向图：writer 包括 token embedding、每个 attention head 的 residual contribution、每个 MLP 输出；reader 包括后续 attention/MLP 输入以及最终 logits。边表示某个 writer 对下游 reader 的信息通路。与常见直接置零不同，作者采用 clean/corrupted prompt 配对，并在关闭边时用 corrupted prompt 上对应 writer 的 counterfactual activation 替代 clean activation，避免把读入分布推离数据流形。这样“边是否必要”更接近因果替换而不是粗暴破坏。

训练上只更新一个从零开始的 LoRA adapter，底座模型冻结。初始时所有边开放。每一轮先用因果重要性估计给当前存活边排序，然后剪掉一批最弱边；接着在新边掩码下训练 LoRA，使适配后的模型尽量复现原模型输出。这里的目标不是重新学任务标签，而是通过剩余通路逼近原模型 next-token distribution，因此它更像 constrained distillation。随后用 held-out controller 决定是否接受本轮剪枝：只有当目标行为准确率仍达标，且 off-target 语言建模能力没有明显恶化时，才保留这次删除，否则回退到上一状态。这个 accept/reject 机制很重要，因为它把“更小电路”约束在“行为保真 + 通用能力保真”之内，而不是单纯追求 sparsity。

作者把最终最小可接受电路记为 C1。与 frozen search 的区别在于：传统 EAP/EAP-IG/EAP-GP 之类方法只是在固定权重上估计边重要性；这里则允许通过低秩参数更新把行为迁移到更少边上。论文还专门做了一个对照：使用同样的搜索流程但不更新权重，结果明显更差，说明收益主要来自 reshaping behavior implementation，而不是搜索器本身更聪明。

另一个技术点是 fidelity 评估不只看任务准确率，还看与原模型输出分布的 KL，以及 condensed circuit 对原模型失败模式的预测能力。也就是说，作者知道“压缩后的小电路”可能只解释修改后的网络，因此专门测试它是否仍能描述原始未修改模型。这一点让方法不只是做出一个小模型，而是尽量保留对原模型机制的解释联系。4a:T8f5,The method is an iterative prune-heal-backtrack procedure. A transformer is represented as a directed graph over residual-stream writers and readers. Writers include token embeddings, each attention head’s residual contribution, and each MLP output; readers include downstream attention/MLP inputs and final logits. Edges represent information flow from an upstream writer to a downstream reader.

A key design choice is how edges are ablated. Instead of zeroing an edge, the method uses paired clean/corrupted prompts and replaces the clean activation flowing through a closed edge with the corresponding activation from the corrupted prompt. This counterfactual replacement avoids pushing activations off-manifold, making edge necessity tests more causally meaningful than naive zero ablation.

Training updates only a freshly initialized LoRA adapter while keeping the base model frozen. Starting from all edges open, each round ranks surviving edges by causal importance, removes the weakest ones, and then trains the LoRA adapter to reproduce the original model’s outputs through the remaining graph. This is closer to constrained distillation than task finetuning: the goal is to match the original next-token distribution under a restricted internal pathway. A held-out controller then accepts the deletion only if both target-task accuracy and off-target language-modeling capability remain within tolerance; otherwise the method rolls back. The smallest accepted graph is denoted C1.

This differs sharply from frozen circuit search methods such as EAP-IG: those estimate importance under fixed weights, whereas Circuit Condensation allows low-rank adaptation to reshape where the behavior lives. The paper explicitly tests the same search loop without weight updates and finds that it loses badly, which isolates the gain to behavioral reshaping rather than better search alone.

Another strong point is the fidelity analysis. The authors do not stop at task accuracy on the adapted model. They also measure KL to the original model’s next-token distribution and test whether the condensed circuit predicts failure modes of the unmodified model better than size-matched frozen circuits. This is important because otherwise one could end up explaining only the modified network.4b:T6f6,The evaluation spans 4 behaviors, 8 models from 4 families, and 3 random seeds. Candidate edge counts range from 2,041 in GPT-2 to 43,993 in Qwen3-4B. The headline result is that in 30 of 32 settings, C1 is smaller than the strongest frozen EAP-IG baseline at comparable task accuracy, by 8.1x on average and up to 316x. The sign test gives p = 2.5e-7, with a bootstrap interval of [4.9, 13.7]. Running the same search without weight updates loses in 29 of 32 settings, supporting the claim that the benefit comes from reshaping the implementation of the behavior.

The paper then shows why smaller circuits matter operationally. The condensed circuits make exhaustive subset tests, all-pairs ablations, and edge-level necessity checks feasible. Matched frozen circuits would require a median 55x more pairwise ablations, and subset enumeration is infeasible in all 19 matched cases reported. So the size reduction changes what verification questions can actually be computed.

On fidelity, the median KL to the original model’s next-token distribution is 0.215 across the grid, and 0.056 on the IOI anchor task, compared with 0.50 for a size-matched frozen circuit. The condensed circuits also better predict where the original model will fail than frozen circuits of the same size. That is a strong indication that the method is not merely producing a compact explanation for the adapted model, but retaining explanatory contact with the original one.

The paper also discusses limits: not every behavior condenses equally well, and some appear to resist concentration under capability constraints. Those failure cases are important because they speak to whether the method is exposing genuine redundancy or simply exploiting the flexibility of LoRA to rewrite local mechanisms.4c:T4dd,For a quick read, focus on three things. First, read the introduction and the main method figure to internalize the objective: the paper is not trying to recover the one true frozen circuit, but to move a behavior into a smaller circuit under fidelity constraints. Second, read the sections defining edge gating, clean/corrupted activation replacement, LoRA-only healing, and the held-out accept/reject controller. Those choices are what make the method more than standard pruning. Third, inspect the results on circuit size, exhaustive verifiability, and fidelity to the original model.
